English for Work · Health & life sciences

Nutrition and Dietetics English

Practical English for dietary histories, food labels, culturally responsive meal planning, nutrient discussions, supplement questions, referrals, and follow-up.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For nutritionists, registered dietitians, nutrition educators, community nutrition staff.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Use neutral follow-up questions to distinguish eating patterns from assumptions about motivation.
  • Explain label arithmetic and distinguish included nutrients from additional amounts.
  • Negotiate feasible food options without dismissing cultural preferences or access constraints.
  • Explain nutrient-source terminology while distinguishing dietary preference from a verified deficiency.
  • Distinguish a referral question, a reported laboratory flag, and the conclusions an assessment can support.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Nutrition and Dietetics English

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Taking a dietary history without judgment02 Explaining serving sizes and label comparisons03 Adapting plans to food access and culture04 Discussing nutrient sources without a one-food solution05 Clarifying a clinical referral and assessment06 Handling supplement claims and interaction questions07 Agreeing on a small, measurable change08 Coordinating follow-up and practical food support
Prompts

All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, a diet prescription, or clinical certification. Titles, credentials, and permission to provide medical nutrition therapy differ by jurisdiction. Use qualified clinical review for disease treatment, suspected deficiency, allergies, eating disorders, supplement interactions, and urgent symptoms. Do not calculate a dose or infer a diagnosis from these exercises. Protect client information and follow actual local procedures.

01Taking a dietary history without judgmentLunch depends on the break+

Your goal

Use neutral follow-up questions to distinguish eating patterns from assumptions about motivation.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Lunch depends on the break

Rina works rotating warehouse shifts. On yesterday's late shift she ate toast before work, had no scheduled meal break, bought noodles after work, and drank two mugs of tea. She cannot recall the mug size. Maya, a nutrition professional, is collecting a dietary history, not diagnosing a deficiency or prescribing a diet. Rina says a typical week is different from yesterday.

Maya: Nutrition professional / Rina: Client

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which record is being discussed first?

Answer and explanation

B. Yesterday's intake

Rina describes one day and explicitly says other days differ.

2. What explains the missed meal in this account?

Answer and explanation

B. No scheduled meal break

The supplied explanation is the work schedule, not a clinical finding.

3. Which quantity remains uncertain?

Answer and explanation

B. The size of each mug

Rina remembers two mugs but cannot specify their volume.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

One recalled day cannot establish the usual pattern. A missing meal does not identify its cause until the client explains the circumstances.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
dietary history
Account of usual food and drink intake and relevant circumstances.take a dietary history
24-hour recall
Structured account of food and drink consumed during a specified day.complete a 24-hour recall
meal pattern
Timing and arrangement of eating occasions.describe the meal pattern
shift work
Work scheduled outside or across conventional daytime hours.accommodate rotating shift work
eating occasion
Any episode of consuming food or drink.identify each eating occasion
portion estimate
Approximate amount actually consumed.clarify a portion estimate
household measure
Familiar container or utensil used to describe an amount.use a household measure
beverage intake
Drinks consumed during the relevant period.record beverage intake
snacking
Eating between the person's main meals.ask about snacking
appetite
Desire to eat, distinct from the opportunity to eat.describe changes in appetite
satiety
Sense of fullness after eating.describe satiety after a meal
meal omission
Absence of a meal from the eating pattern.explore a meal omission
recall bias
Inaccuracy arising from what someone remembers or reports.acknowledge recall bias
usual intake
Eating pattern over time rather than one isolated day.distinguish usual intake
weekend variation
Difference between weekday and weekend routines.explore weekend variation
preparation method
Way a food is cooked or otherwise prepared.clarify the preparation method
food record
Account of foods and drinks recorded over a period.review a food record
neutral probe
Nonjudgmental follow-up question that elicits detail.use a neutral probe
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Keep uncertain quantities approximate. Two mugs is a count, not a verified volume, and no nutrient deficiency follows from this brief account.

C Notice the language

Curiosity before judgment

Ask about practical circumstances. Words such as cheats, bad food, and failed imply a judgment that the supplied history does not support.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open neutrally
Could you walk me through what you ate yesterday?
Clarify timing
Was that before your shift or after you got home?
Explore access
What happens when there is no meal break?
Clarify quantity
Which cup or container did you use?
Allow uncertainty
An approximate amount is useful; you do not need to guess an exact number.
Check representativeness
How does that compare with a more usual day?
Avoid judgment
I am trying to understand the routine, not grade your choices.
Summarize accurately
The missed meal was linked to the break schedule.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Usually versus yesterday
Usually describes a pattern; yesterday identifies one day. Preserve the distinction when summarizing intake.
No break versus no appetite
One describes access to time; the other describes desire to eat. Neither can replace the other without evidence.
About
About marks a quantity as approximate, preventing a remembered estimate from looking measured.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which follow-up best tests whether yesterday was typical?

Answer and explanation

C. How does that compare with your other shifts?

Comparing shifts checks representativeness without assuming a pattern or motive.

2. Which summary preserves the uncertainty?

Answer and explanation

D. Two mugs; volume not recalled

The count is known, while container volume is not supplied.

3. Which wording is least judgmental?

Answer and explanation

D. What made a meal difficult during that shift?

This question asks about circumstances without alleging failure or misconduct.

4. Which distinction matters when interpreting this record?

Answer and explanation

A. Intake on one day versus usual intake

The central limitation is that one day's account cannot establish the usual pattern.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Lunch depends on the break

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Maya: Nutrition professional / Rina: Client

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • usual intake
  • 24-hour recall
  • beverage intake
  • food record
  • appetite
  • neutral probe
  • portion estimate
  • shift work
  • meal pattern
  • household measure
  1. Maya
    Could you walk me through yesterday, starting with what you had before your shift?
  2. Rina
    Toast and tea. I can manage this 1, but yesterday was not a normal day for me.
  3. Maya
    That is useful to know. We will compare it with other days after we get the sequence clear.
  4. Rina
    My 2 rotates, so breakfast sometimes happens when other people are having dinner.
  5. Maya
    When you said you missed lunch, did you mean you were not hungry or could not take a break?
  6. Rina
    There was no meal break. My 3 was fine; I simply could not leave the floor.
  7. Maya
    Thanks for separating those. What was the next thing you ate, including anything between meals?
  8. Rina
    Noodles after work. My 4 would be one takeout container, although I left some broth.
  9. Maya
    Do you remember how full it was, or would the restaurant's container help us describe it?
  10. Rina
    A container would help. A 5 is easier than trying to guess the weight.
  11. Maya
    And drinks across the whole shift, not only the drink that came with your food?
  12. Rina
    Two mugs of tea. That is my remembered 6, but I cannot tell you the mug size.
  13. Maya
    I will leave the volume uncertain. Was there anything small that we have not mentioned?
  14. Rina
    No, not yesterday. My 7 is different when I work mornings.
  15. Maya
    Then we should not turn yesterday into a description of every week. What changes on mornings?
  16. Rina
    I get a proper break. My 8 includes a meal at work, not just food after the shift.
  17. Maya
    That helps. I am understanding the routine rather than deciding whether you made good or bad choices.
  18. Rina
    I appreciate that. A 9 feels easier to answer than asking why I skipped food again.
  19. Maya
    We can review a few different days next time using the record format we agree on.
  20. Rina
    I can bring a 10 from both shift types, without pretending that each amount was measured.

Answers and explanations
  1. 24-hour recall Turn 2. A 24-hour recall concerns a specified day, not an assumed average across the week.
  2. shift work Turn 4. Shift work changes the timing of work and can affect opportunities to eat.
  3. appetite Turn 6. Appetite concerns the desire to eat, which is different from having time or access to food.
  4. portion estimate Turn 8. A portion estimate describes the approximate amount actually eaten rather than a prescribed amount.
  5. household measure Turn 10. A household measure uses a familiar container or utensil to make an amount easier to describe.
  6. beverage intake Turn 12. Beverage intake includes drinks across the stated period, not just drinks taken with meals.
  7. meal pattern Turn 14. A meal pattern describes the timing and arrangement of eating occasions rather than judging food choices.
  8. usual intake Turn 16. Usual intake is the pattern over time and cannot be established from one unusual day alone.
  9. neutral probe Turn 18. A neutral probe elicits detail without implying blame or assuming why the person acted.
  10. food record Turn 20. A food record documents foods and drinks over a stated period and can capture differences between shifts.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Maya: Could you walk me through yesterday, starting with what you had before your shift?
  2. Rina: Toast and tea. I can manage this 24-hour recall, but yesterday was not a normal day for me.
  3. Maya: That is useful to know. We will compare it with other days after we get the sequence clear.
  4. Rina: My shift work rotates, so breakfast sometimes happens when other people are having dinner.
  5. Maya: When you said you missed lunch, did you mean you were not hungry or could not take a break?
  6. Rina: There was no meal break. My appetite was fine; I simply could not leave the floor.
  7. Maya: Thanks for separating those. What was the next thing you ate, including anything between meals?
  8. Rina: Noodles after work. My portion estimate would be one takeout container, although I left some broth.
  9. Maya: Do you remember how full it was, or would the restaurant's container help us describe it?
  10. Rina: A container would help. A household measure is easier than trying to guess the weight.
  11. Maya: And drinks across the whole shift, not only the drink that came with your food?
  12. Rina: Two mugs of tea. That is my remembered beverage intake, but I cannot tell you the mug size.
  13. Maya: I will leave the volume uncertain. Was there anything small that we have not mentioned?
  14. Rina: No, not yesterday. My meal pattern is different when I work mornings.
  15. Maya: Then we should not turn yesterday into a description of every week. What changes on mornings?
  16. Rina: I get a proper break. My usual intake includes a meal at work, not just food after the shift.
  17. Maya: That helps. I am understanding the routine rather than deciding whether you made good or bad choices.
  18. Rina: I appreciate that. A neutral probe feels easier to answer than asking why I skipped food again.
  19. Maya: We can review a few different days next time using the record format we agree on.
  20. Rina: I can bring a food record from both shift types, without pretending that each amount was measured.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Breakfast happens after midnight

A night worker clarifies the meaning of a meal name.

  1. Client: When I wrote breakfast, I meant the meal after waking, which was just after midnight.
  2. Nutrition professional: Thank you. Let us record the actual time as well as the meal name.
  3. Client: Otherwise it looks as though I had nothing between the evening and the next morning.
  4. Nutrition professional: Exactly. Conventional meal names can hide a different routine when someone works overnight.
  5. Client: Would you like the work start time beside the food entry as well?
  6. Nutrition professional: That would help us understand the sequence without assuming a daytime schedule.
  7. Client: The food was the same as usual, but the timing changed that week.
  8. Nutrition professional: I will keep timing and food choice separate when I summarize the account.
  9. Client: I also drank tea during the shift, not with that first meal.
  10. Nutrition professional: Then we should include it at the right point rather than leave beverages out.
A photograph helps but does not weigh the meal

A client offers a food photograph to clarify a portion.

  1. Client: I took a photograph because I could not remember how to describe the container.
  2. Nutrition professional: That can help us discuss it, although the image does not give an exact weight.
  3. Client: The plate looks full, but some of what you see is a folded napkin.
  4. Nutrition professional: That is a useful correction. I should ask what is visible rather than assume.
  5. Client: I also left part of the rice, which the photograph was taken before I ate.
  6. Nutrition professional: Then it shows what was served, not necessarily the amount you actually consumed.
  7. Client: Should I describe the leftovers alongside the photograph when we review the record?
  8. Nutrition professional: Yes, with any uncertainty clear. An estimate is better than invented precision.
  9. Client: I can explain the ingredients, but I do not know their exact proportions.
  10. Nutrition professional: We can record that limitation and avoid treating the image as a laboratory measurement.
Sometimes does not mean every day

A professional checks an ambiguous frequency word.

  1. Nutrition professional: You wrote that you sometimes buy a snack after work. What does sometimes mean here?
  2. Client: Last week it happened twice, but that was different from the week before.
  3. Nutrition professional: That gives us a specific week without turning it into a permanent daily pattern.
  4. Client: I was worried you would read it as something I did after every shift.
  5. Nutrition professional: No. I would rather clarify the frequency than quietly replace your word with daily.
  6. Client: One of those days I had a meal break, and the other day I did not.
  7. Nutrition professional: Then the circumstances differ too. We should not assign the same explanation to both.
  8. Client: I can remember the foods, but not the exact time on the second day.
  9. Nutrition professional: We can keep that time approximate and retain the details you do remember.
  10. Client: That feels like an accurate account instead of a more confident story than I can support.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Lunch depends on the break
  1. Read the corrected exchange and stress yesterday, usual, and approximate.
  2. Change roles and repeat the questions that distinguish appetite from break access.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A cup was not a measuring cup

A client recorded one cup of soup but meant a large mug, not a measured cup. The exact volume remains unknown; the container can be checked next time.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Professional: "Your word cup referred to a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. mug

The client meant a drinking container rather than a standardized measure.

2. Client: "The exact volume is still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unknown

No measurement is supplied, so the precise volume remains uncertain.

3. Professional: "The record needs that ___."

Answer and explanation

B. clarification

Clarifying the container prevents the entry from appearing precisely measured.

4. Client: "Next time I can show you the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. container

Seeing the actual container can help describe the amount more accurately.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Nutrition and Dietetics English
Professional audience: nutritionists, registered dietitians, nutrition educators, community nutrition staff
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, a diet prescription, or clinical certification. Titles, credentials, and permission to provide medical nutrition therapy differ by jurisdiction. Use qualified clinical review for disease treatment, suspected deficiency, allergies, eating disorders, supplement interactions, and urgent symptoms. Do not calculate a dose or infer a diagnosis from these exercises. Protect client information and follow actual local procedures.
Lesson 01: Taking a dietary history without judgment
Original fictional case: Rina works rotating warehouse shifts. On yesterday's late shift she ate toast before work, had no scheduled meal break, bought noodles after work, and drank two mugs of tea. She cannot recall the mug size. Maya, a nutrition professional, is collecting a dietary history, not diagnosing a deficiency or prescribing a diet. Rina says a typical week is different from yesterday.
Language workshop: Lunch depends on the break
Communication goal: Use neutral follow-up questions to distinguish eating patterns from assumptions about motivation.
Language guidance: One recalled day cannot establish the usual pattern. A missing meal does not identify its cause until the client explains the circumstances.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Could you walk me through what you ate yesterday? / Was that before your shift or after you got home? / What happens when there is no meal break? / Which cup or container did you use? / An approximate amount is useful; you do not need to guess an exact number. / How does that compare with a more usual day? / I am trying to understand the routine, not grade your choices. / The missed meal was linked to the break schedule. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: dietary history: Account of usual food and drink intake and relevant circumstances. / 24-hour recall: Structured account of food and drink consumed during a specified day. / meal pattern: Timing and arrangement of eating occasions. / shift work: Work scheduled outside or across conventional daytime hours. / eating occasion: Any episode of consuming food or drink. / portion estimate: Approximate amount actually consumed. / household measure: Familiar container or utensil used to describe an amount. / beverage intake: Drinks consumed during the relevant period. / snacking: Eating between the person's main meals. / appetite: Desire to eat, distinct from the opportunity to eat. / satiety: Sense of fullness after eating. / meal omission: Absence of a meal from the eating pattern. / recall bias: Inaccuracy arising from what someone remembers or reports. / usual intake: Eating pattern over time rather than one isolated day. / weekend variation: Difference between weekday and weekend routines. / preparation method: Way a food is cooked or otherwise prepared. / food record: Account of foods and drinks recorded over a period. / neutral probe: Nonjudgmental follow-up question that elicits detail. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange and stress yesterday, usual, and approximate. Change roles and repeat the questions that distinguish appetite from break access. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Maya: Nutrition professional / Rina: Client
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Maya: Nutrition professional / Rina: Client

Rina: I appreciate that. A neutral probe feels easier to answer than asking why I skipped food again.
Maya: We can review a few different days next time using the record format we agree on.
Rina: I can bring a food record from both shift types, without pretending that each amount was measured.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Nutrition and Dietetics English
Professional audience: nutritionists, registered dietitians, nutrition educators, community nutrition staff
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, a diet prescription, or clinical certification. Titles, credentials, and permission to provide medical nutrition therapy differ by jurisdiction. Use qualified clinical review for disease treatment, suspected deficiency, allergies, eating disorders, supplement interactions, and urgent symptoms. Do not calculate a dose or infer a diagnosis from these exercises. Protect client information and follow actual local procedures.
Lesson 01: Taking a dietary history without judgment
Original fictional case: Rina works rotating warehouse shifts. On yesterday's late shift she ate toast before work, had no scheduled meal break, bought noodles after work, and drank two mugs of tea. She cannot recall the mug size. Maya, a nutrition professional, is collecting a dietary history, not diagnosing a deficiency or prescribing a diet. Rina says a typical week is different from yesterday.
Language workshop: Lunch depends on the break
Communication goal: Use neutral follow-up questions to distinguish eating patterns from assumptions about motivation.
Language guidance: One recalled day cannot establish the usual pattern. A missing meal does not identify its cause until the client explains the circumstances.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Could you walk me through what you ate yesterday? / Was that before your shift or after you got home? / What happens when there is no meal break? / Which cup or container did you use? / An approximate amount is useful; you do not need to guess an exact number. / How does that compare with a more usual day? / I am trying to understand the routine, not grade your choices. / The missed meal was linked to the break schedule. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.

02Explaining serving sizes and label comparisonsThe package contains two servings+

Your goal

Explain label arithmetic and distinguish included nutrients from additional amounts.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The package contains two servings

An invented yogurt package contains two servings. Per serving, its label lists 12 grams of total sugars, including 5 grams of added sugars. Jordan eats the whole package. Nutrition educator Lee explains the printed figures without setting a personal sugar target. A second product uses a different serving weight, so a direct per-serving comparison may be misleading.

Jordan: Client / Lee: Nutrition educator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many servings did Jordan eat?

Answer and explanation

D. Two

Jordan ate the entire package, which contains two stated servings.

2. How much added sugar was in the whole package?

Answer and explanation

C. 10 grams

Two servings multiplied by five grams gives ten grams of added sugars.

3. Why should the second product's serving weight be checked?

Answer and explanation

B. The reference amounts differ

Different reference amounts can make a per-serving comparison misleading.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The five grams of added sugars are included in the twelve grams of total sugars per serving. Do not add them again. The whole package contains twenty-four grams total, including ten grams added.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
Nutrition Facts panel
Standardized nutrition information displayed on a US food label.read the Nutrition Facts panel
serving size
Label reference amount used for the listed nutrient figures.check the serving size
servings per container
Number of label servings within the package.multiply by servings per container
total sugars
All sugars included in the stated label amount.compare total sugars
added sugars
Sugars added during processing or preparation under the relevant labeling rules.identify added sugars
naturally occurring sugars
Sugars present in the food's ingredients without being added as sweeteners.distinguish naturally occurring sugars
percent Daily Value
Label percentage showing contribution to a general daily reference amount.interpret percent Daily Value
dual-column label
Panel presenting nutrition information for two stated reference amounts.read a dual-column label
reference amount
Quantity against which a label or comparison is expressed.align the reference amounts
per-serving value
Nutrient amount for one stated label serving.use the per-serving value
per-container value
Nutrient amount for the complete package.calculate the per-container value
ingredient list
Ingredients listed under applicable labeling requirements.examine the ingredient list
nutrient density
Nutrient content relative to an amount of food or its energy content.discuss nutrient density
sodium content
Amount of sodium in the specified quantity.compare sodium content
dietary fiber
Food components classified as fiber under applicable definitions.identify dietary fiber
saturated fat
Type of fat reported separately in nutrition labeling.check saturated fat
label rounding
Permitted rounding of figures displayed on a label.allow for label rounding
like-for-like comparison
Comparison using equivalent quantities and definitions.make a like-for-like comparison
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

A label reference amount is not a personalized eating recommendation. Percent Daily Value is also not the percentage of a person's entire diet supplied by that food.

C Notice the language

Numbers need a reference amount

Check whether the listener means one serving or the whole package. Familiar packaging can hide an unfamiliar reference amount.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the reference
These figures are for the serving shown here.
Scale the amount
For the whole package, multiply each per-serving amount by two.
Explain inclusion
Added sugars are included in total sugars, not added on top.
Compare fairly
Let us put both labels on the same quantity basis.
Avoid prescription
The label does not set your individual portion.
Clarify percentage
This percentage refers to the label's daily reference value.
Check the container
How much of the package did you actually eat?
Correct arithmetic
The total is twenty-four, not thirty-four grams.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Including
Including marks a subset: added sugars form part of total sugars, not a separate amount to add again.
Per
Per identifies the denominator or reference amount. Per serving and per package can produce different figures.
Twice as much
Twice as much scales an amount by two; it does not mean two grams more.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement explains the whole package correctly?

Answer and explanation

D. 24 grams total sugars, including 10 grams added

Both per-serving amounts double, while added sugars remain part of total sugars.

2. What does including signal?

Answer and explanation

B. A quantity within the total

Including identifies a subset and prevents double-counting the same nutrient amount.

3. Which comparison is most defensible?

Answer and explanation

D. Compare equivalent amounts before interpreting the figures

Equivalent reference amounts are necessary for a meaningful quantity comparison.

4. Which statement avoids turning the label into a prescription?

Answer and explanation

D. This is the label's reference serving, not your individualized portion

The label reports nutrition information and does not prescribe an individual's intake.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

The package contains two servings

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jordan: Client / Lee: Nutrition educator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • like-for-like comparison
  • servings per container
  • added sugars
  • serving size
  • reference amount
  • Nutrition Facts panel
  • Percent Daily Value
  • ingredient list
  • per-container value
  • per-serving value
  1. Jordan
    I thought the yogurt had twelve grams of sugar, but my colleague says it had thirty-four.
  2. Lee
    Let us check the 1 together before deciding which calculation fits what you ate.
  3. Jordan
    I ate the entire package. The front of it looked like something sold for one person.
  4. Lee
    The 2 entry says two, so the printed per-serving amounts need to be doubled here.
  5. Jordan
    That would make the twelve grams twenty-four. Where does the extra five fit into the calculation?
  6. Lee
    Those are 3. The word including means they are already part of the twelve, not an extra five.
  7. Jordan
    Then the complete package contains ten grams added, inside the twenty-four grams total. Is that right?
  8. Lee
    Exactly. You have kept the 4 separate from the amount listed for one serving.
  9. Jordan
    Does that mean the label is telling me I was supposed to eat only half the package?
  10. Lee
    No. The 5 explains the figures; it does not determine your individual portion or nutritional needs.
  11. Jordan
    The other yogurt looks lower in sugar per serving. Could I compare those two printed numbers directly?
  12. Lee
    First check the 6. Its stated serving is a different weight, so the numbers are not yet comparable.
  13. Jordan
    We would need equivalent amounts, not just two columns that happen to use the word serving.
  14. Lee
    Yes, a 7 keeps the quantity consistent before interpreting the difference between products.
  15. Jordan
    What about the percentage beside the number? I assumed that meant a percentage of everything I ate today.
  16. Lee
    8 uses a general daily reference. It is not a measurement of your actual diet today.
  17. Jordan
    I also noticed an ingredient list. Would that answer a different question from these nutrient amounts?
  18. Lee
    Yes. The 9 tells you what the food contains, while this panel gives the specified nutrient figures.
  19. Jordan
    I understand now: check how much I ate, identify the reference, and avoid adding the included sugars twice.
  20. Lee
    Correct. Keep the 10 attached to its serving whenever you explain the calculation to someone else.

Answers and explanations
  1. Nutrition Facts panel Turn 2. The Nutrition Facts panel provides the serving reference and nutrient amounts needed to resolve the disagreement.
  2. servings per container Turn 4. Servings per container tells us that the full package contains two of the label's reference servings.
  3. added sugars Turn 6. Added sugars are included within the stated total sugars and must not be counted a second time.
  4. per-container value Turn 8. The per-container value describes the whole package, unlike the figures printed for one serving.
  5. serving size Turn 10. Serving size supplies a reference for the label figures and is not an individualized prescription for the client.
  6. reference amount Turn 12. The reference amount is the quantity behind each number, and unequal quantities can distort a comparison.
  7. like-for-like comparison Turn 14. A like-for-like comparison uses equivalent quantities and definitions so the difference has a meaningful basis.
  8. Percent Daily Value Turn 16. Percent Daily Value uses a general label reference amount rather than measuring the person's actual intake that day.
  9. ingredient list Turn 18. The ingredient list identifies ingredients, while the nutrition panel reports amounts of specified nutrients.
  10. per-serving value Turn 20. The per-serving value applies to one stated label serving and must be scaled when a different amount is eaten.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jordan: I thought the yogurt had twelve grams of sugar, but my colleague says it had thirty-four.
  2. Lee: Let us check the Nutrition Facts panel together before deciding which calculation fits what you ate.
  3. Jordan: I ate the entire package. The front of it looked like something sold for one person.
  4. Lee: The servings per container entry says two, so the printed per-serving amounts need to be doubled here.
  5. Jordan: That would make the twelve grams twenty-four. Where does the extra five fit into the calculation?
  6. Lee: Those are added sugars. The word including means they are already part of the twelve, not an extra five.
  7. Jordan: Then the complete package contains ten grams added, inside the twenty-four grams total. Is that right?
  8. Lee: Exactly. You have kept the per-container value separate from the amount listed for one serving.
  9. Jordan: Does that mean the label is telling me I was supposed to eat only half the package?
  10. Lee: No. The serving size explains the figures; it does not determine your individual portion or nutritional needs.
  11. Jordan: The other yogurt looks lower in sugar per serving. Could I compare those two printed numbers directly?
  12. Lee: First check the reference amount. Its stated serving is a different weight, so the numbers are not yet comparable.
  13. Jordan: We would need equivalent amounts, not just two columns that happen to use the word serving.
  14. Lee: Yes, a like-for-like comparison keeps the quantity consistent before interpreting the difference between products.
  15. Jordan: What about the percentage beside the number? I assumed that meant a percentage of everything I ate today.
  16. Lee: Percent Daily Value uses a general daily reference. It is not a measurement of your actual diet today.
  17. Jordan: I also noticed an ingredient list. Would that answer a different question from these nutrient amounts?
  18. Lee: Yes. The ingredient list tells you what the food contains, while this panel gives the specified nutrient figures.
  19. Jordan: I understand now: check how much I ate, identify the reference, and avoid adding the included sugars twice.
  20. Lee: Correct. Keep the per-serving value attached to its serving whenever you explain the calculation to someone else.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The package changed its serving count

A client compares an old label photograph with a current package.

  1. Client: I used last month's photograph to calculate the amount in this new package.
  2. Educator: Let us check whether the current package has the same serving size and serving count.
  3. Client: The old one held two servings, but this one lists three smaller servings.
  4. Educator: Then the old calculation cannot simply be carried over because the reference changed.
  5. Client: The product name is identical, which is why I assumed the figures were identical too.
  6. Educator: A familiar name does not guarantee that the package or label information is unchanged.
  7. Client: We should use the current label and the amount I actually ate today.
  8. Educator: Exactly. State both before multiplying the figures so someone else can follow the calculation.
  9. Client: I will keep the old photograph as historical information rather than today's source.
  10. Educator: That makes the comparison traceable without treating an outdated package as the current one.
Lower sodium needs an equal quantity

Two colleagues compare invented label figures.

  1. Colleague: Product A lists less sodium per serving, so I called it lower in sodium overall.
  2. Nutrition educator: What quantities do the two serving sizes represent in the labels you are comparing?
  3. Colleague: One is a small portion and the other is twice that amount by weight.
  4. Nutrition educator: Then compare equivalent quantities before deciding what the numbers mean for the same amount eaten.
  5. Colleague: I should not let the word serving make different quantities look automatically comparable.
  6. Nutrition educator: Correct. The comparison needs a shared reference, not merely similarly placed numbers on packages.
  7. Colleague: Would package price tell us which product has the lower sodium content?
  8. Nutrition educator: No. Price answers a different question and cannot replace the nutrient information.
  9. Colleague: I will revise the summary to identify the amount used for each figure.
  10. Nutrition educator: That will make the comparison understandable and prevent a confident but misleading shortcut.
Including is not plus

A learner corrects a label explanation.

  1. Client: I told my partner to add total sugars and added sugars to find the real total.
  2. Educator: The word including changes that calculation: added sugars are already part of total sugars.
  3. Client: So I counted the added part twice by treating it as a separate extra quantity.
  4. Educator: Yes. Keep the subset inside the total rather than adding it again.
  5. Client: Does that change when I eat two servings instead of one serving?
  6. Educator: Both figures scale to two servings, but the inclusion relationship stays the same.
  7. Client: Then I can report the total and say how much of it was added.
  8. Educator: Exactly. That wording preserves the relationship without asking the listener to infer it.
  9. Client: I will correct the earlier explanation rather than leave two contradictory calculations circulating.
  10. Educator: Good. A brief corrected example is clearer than simply saying the old answer was wrong.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The package contains two servings
  1. Read the corrected exchange, emphasizing per serving, whole package, and including.
  2. Swap roles and state the two correct totals without turning the label into a personal target.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Three servings in a bottle

An invented bottle contains three servings. Each serving lists 4 grams total sugars, including 1 gram added. A client drinks the entire bottle. No personal target is supplied.

Answer and explanation

B. three

The package explicitly lists three servings, all of which were consumed.

2. Educator: "The whole bottle has ___ grams of total sugars."

Answer and explanation

D. twelve

Three servings multiplied by four grams gives twelve grams total.

3. Client: "Added sugars are ___ in that total."

Answer and explanation

B. included

Added sugars are a subset of total sugars, not an extra amount.

4. Educator: "The label does not set a personal ___."

Answer and explanation

D. target

The scenario gives label quantities but no individualized nutrition target.

Vocabulary: Nutrition Facts panel: Standardized nutrition information displayed on a US food label. / serving size: Label reference amount used for the listed nutrient figures. / servings per container: Number of label servings within the package. / total sugars: All sugars included in the stated label amount. / added sugars: Sugars added during processing or preparation under the relevant labeling rules. / naturally occurring sugars: Sugars present in the food's ingredients without being added as sweeteners. / percent Daily Value: Label percentage showing contribution to a general daily reference amount. / dual-column label: Panel presenting nutrition information for two stated reference amounts. / reference amount: Quantity against which a label or comparison is expressed. / per-serving value: Nutrient amount for one stated label serving. / per-container value: Nutrient amount for the complete package. / ingredient list: Ingredients listed under applicable labeling requirements. / nutrient density: Nutrient content relative to an amount of food or its energy content. / sodium content: Amount of sodium in the specified quantity. / dietary fiber: Food components classified as fiber under applicable definitions. / saturated fat: Type of fat reported separately in nutrition labeling. / label rounding: Permitted rounding of figures displayed on a label. / like-for-like comparison: Comparison using equivalent quantities and definitions. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Adapting plans to food access and cultureKeep the rice; fix the logistics+

Your goal

Negotiate feasible food options without dismissing cultural preferences or access constraints.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Keep the rice; fix the logistics

Ana shares a kitchen and has one refrigerator shelf. She wants to keep rice and beans in her meals. Her food budget is limited, the nearest shop is a small market, and she has no freezer space. Nutrition professional Sam discusses two feasible shopping options with her. No clinical restriction or individualized nutrient target is supplied.

Ana: Client / Sam: Nutrition professional

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which food preference is explicit?

Answer and explanation

A. Keeping rice and beans

Ana explicitly wants to preserve rice and beans in her meals.

2. Which storage option is unavailable?

Answer and explanation

A. Freezer space

The brief states that Ana has no freezer space available.

3. What kind of decision is being discussed?

Answer and explanation

D. Feasible shopping options

The discussion concerns access and feasibility, not an individualized clinical prescription.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A cheaper large package is not necessarily the practical choice when storage is limited. Do not treat a preferred staple as the problem simply because it is unfamiliar to the professional.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
food access
Practical ability to obtain suitable food.assess food access
food insecurity
Limited or uncertain access to adequate food.discuss food insecurity sensitively
food budget
Money available for food over a stated period.work within a food budget
staple food
Regularly eaten food central to a person's meals.retain a familiar staple food
cultural preference
Food choice connected with personal or community traditions.respect a cultural preference
shared kitchen
Cooking space used by more than one household or person.plan around a shared kitchen
storage capacity
Space and conditions available for keeping food.check storage capacity
shelf-stable
Able to be stored unopened without refrigeration under stated conditions.choose a shelf-stable option
perishable
Likely to spoil unless stored and used appropriately.plan for perishable ingredients
batch cooking
Preparing several portions in one cooking session.discuss batch cooking realistically
food waste
Food discarded rather than consumed.reduce avoidable food waste
unit price
Cost expressed for a standard quantity.compare unit prices
seasonal produce
Fruits or vegetables available in their relevant growing season.compare seasonal produce
substitution
Replacement chosen for a particular purpose or constraint.agree on a feasible substitution
preparation time
Time needed to make a food ready to eat.estimate preparation time
equipment access
Availability of the tools needed for preparation.establish equipment access
community food resource
Local service supporting access to food.identify a community food resource
acceptability
Whether an option fits the person's preferences and circumstances.check meal acceptability
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Shelf-stable describes storage under stated conditions, usually before opening; check the actual product instructions. This conversation does not authorize ignoring food-safety guidance.

C Notice the language

Familiar food is information, not an obstacle

A culturally responsive discussion asks what the meal means and what is available. Do not assume that changing cuisine is the goal.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Preserve preference
Which familiar foods would you like to keep?
Ask about equipment
What can you actually use in the shared kitchen?
Check storage
Would there be space to store that after opening?
Compare costs
The unit price is lower, but the upfront cost is higher.
Offer a choice
Which of these two options fits your week better?
Respect limits
A plan that needs a freezer would not fit your situation.
Ask permission
Would you like information about local food resources?
Confirm feasibility
Let us check cost, storage, and preparation time together.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Would fit
Would fit presents compatibility with the stated circumstances rather than a moral judgment about the choice.
Cheaper per unit
A lower unit price does not imply a lower total purchase cost or less waste.
Prefer versus need
Prefer identifies a choice; need may identify a constraint. Ask which meaning the speaker intends.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which suggestion best respects the stated storage limit?

Answer and explanation

D. Compare manageable packages and shelf-stable options

Manageable packages and appropriate shelf-stable options can be discussed without assuming unavailable freezer space.

2. Which question checks acceptability?

Answer and explanation

A. Which option would you actually want to eat?

Acceptability concerns the client's preferences, not conformity to the professional's meals.

3. What does a lower unit price establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Lower cost for the comparison quantity

Unit price compares equivalent quantities but does not settle total cost or practicality.

4. Which resource offer preserves choice?

Answer and explanation

C. Would you like information about local food support?

Asking permission offers practical support without making an unsupported judgment or decision.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Keep the rice; fix the logistics

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Client / Sam: Nutrition professional

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • food waste
  • shelf-stable
  • community food resource
  • equipment access
  • storage capacity
  • acceptability
  • unit price
  • staple food
  • cultural preference
  • Batch cooking
  1. Ana
    I brought the suggested shopping list, but I cannot store half of it in our shared kitchen.
  2. Sam
    Let us start with your 1 instead of assuming that everyone has a full refrigerator and freezer.
  3. Ana
    One refrigerator shelf, no freezer space, and a cupboard. I also want to keep rice and beans.
  4. Sam
    We can keep that 2 combination in the discussion. Which ingredients do you normally buy at the nearby market?
  5. Ana
    Small bags of rice and canned beans. The bigger packages look cheaper, but the checkout total matters.
  6. Sam
    The 3 may be lower for a large package while the upfront cost is higher.
  7. Ana
    Exactly. I also cannot keep a giant opened container cold just because it was good value.
  8. Sam
    Then 4 options still need a check of what happens after opening. We should use the actual instructions.
  9. Ana
    I can cook twice a week, but the kitchen is busy most evenings and I have only one saucepan.
  10. Sam
    That 5 matters. A plan requiring three pans at once would not fit the routine you described.
  11. Ana
    People keep suggesting batch cooking, but they seem to imagine a freezer full of labeled containers.
  12. Sam
    6 is only useful when preparation and safe storage are feasible. We do not have to build around it.
  13. Ana
    Could we compare smaller packages of fresh vegetables with the canned options at my market?
  14. Sam
    Yes, and check 7 too. Which would you actually enjoy alongside your rice and beans?
  15. Ana
    I would use both, depending on the price. I dislike buying a large bag and throwing most away.
  16. Sam
    Then avoiding 8 belongs in the comparison, alongside the price and what you can store safely.
  17. Ana
    That feels more realistic. I am not trying to change everything I eat or buy special ingredients.
  18. Sam
    Your 9 is part of the plan. We are adapting the logistics, not replacing your familiar meals.
  19. Ana
    I would also like information about local support, as long as asking does not commit me to anything.
  20. Sam
    I can explain a 10 and its actual access arrangements. You can decide whether you would like to follow up.

Answers and explanations
  1. storage capacity Turn 2. Storage capacity concerns the actual space and conditions available for food, which constrain a feasible shopping plan.
  2. staple food Turn 4. A staple food is central to familiar meals and need not be removed merely because the professional suggests changes.
  3. unit price Turn 6. Unit price compares cost for a standard quantity and does not by itself describe the full amount paid at checkout.
  4. shelf-stable Turn 8. Shelf-stable describes storage under specified conditions, and opening the package can change the storage requirements.
  5. equipment access Turn 10. Equipment access identifies the preparation tools actually available rather than assuming a fully equipped private kitchen.
  6. Batch cooking Turn 12. Batch cooking means preparing multiple portions together, but its feasibility depends on safe storage and available space.
  7. acceptability Turn 14. Acceptability asks whether the available option suits the person's preferences and circumstances, not merely whether it is available.
  8. food waste Turn 16. Food waste is food discarded rather than eaten, which can make a seemingly economical purchase impractical.
  9. cultural preference Turn 18. A cultural preference informs the plan and should not be treated as an obstacle or a defect in the client.
  10. community food resource Turn 20. A community food resource is a local support option whose access arrangements and suitability should be checked with the client.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ana: I brought the suggested shopping list, but I cannot store half of it in our shared kitchen.
  2. Sam: Let us start with your storage capacity instead of assuming that everyone has a full refrigerator and freezer.
  3. Ana: One refrigerator shelf, no freezer space, and a cupboard. I also want to keep rice and beans.
  4. Sam: We can keep that staple food combination in the discussion. Which ingredients do you normally buy at the nearby market?
  5. Ana: Small bags of rice and canned beans. The bigger packages look cheaper, but the checkout total matters.
  6. Sam: The unit price may be lower for a large package while the upfront cost is higher.
  7. Ana: Exactly. I also cannot keep a giant opened container cold just because it was good value.
  8. Sam: Then shelf-stable options still need a check of what happens after opening. We should use the actual instructions.
  9. Ana: I can cook twice a week, but the kitchen is busy most evenings and I have only one saucepan.
  10. Sam: That equipment access matters. A plan requiring three pans at once would not fit the routine you described.
  11. Ana: People keep suggesting batch cooking, but they seem to imagine a freezer full of labeled containers.
  12. Sam: Batch cooking is only useful when preparation and safe storage are feasible. We do not have to build around it.
  13. Ana: Could we compare smaller packages of fresh vegetables with the canned options at my market?
  14. Sam: Yes, and check acceptability too. Which would you actually enjoy alongside your rice and beans?
  15. Ana: I would use both, depending on the price. I dislike buying a large bag and throwing most away.
  16. Sam: Then avoiding food waste belongs in the comparison, alongside the price and what you can store safely.
  17. Ana: That feels more realistic. I am not trying to change everything I eat or buy special ingredients.
  18. Sam: Your cultural preference is part of the plan. We are adapting the logistics, not replacing your familiar meals.
  19. Ana: I would also like information about local support, as long as asking does not commit me to anything.

Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The kitchen is shared by schedule

A client explains why a cooking suggestion is impractical.

  1. Client: The kitchen exists, but I cannot use it during the evening period you suggested.
  2. Nutrition professional: Then availability is a scheduling question, not simply whether your home has a kitchen.
  3. Client: Other residents use the stove then, and the agreement gives me a later time.
  4. Nutrition professional: We should work with the actual arrangement rather than assume you can move everyone's schedule.
  5. Client: I have a short preparation window before leaving for work on two days.
  6. Nutrition professional: That is useful. Which of the options we discussed fits that window and your available equipment?
  7. Client: The one-pot option fits, but the plan requiring an oven does not.
  8. Nutrition professional: Then we can compare those practical requirements without describing your routine as unwillingness.
  9. Client: I would also like to keep the seasonings my family normally uses.
  10. Nutrition professional: Let us include that preference while checking any actual restrictions supplied by your clinical team.
Bulk buying creates waste

A client evaluates a promotional offer.

  1. Client: The promotion is cheaper per kilogram, but I cannot finish that quantity before it spoils.
  2. Nutrition professional: Then the advertised unit saving is not the only cost relevant to your situation.
  3. Client: The smaller package costs more per kilogram but fits what I can use this week.
  4. Nutrition professional: That may make it more practical. We should compare total cost and avoidable waste too.
  5. Client: I do not have freezer space to solve the problem by storing the remainder.
  6. Nutrition professional: Then freezing cannot be the assumed solution in this particular plan.
  7. Client: Could we keep the smaller package as one option rather than calling it poor value?
  8. Nutrition professional: Yes. Value depends on what you can buy, store safely, and actually use.
  9. Client: I want a realistic shopping discussion, not pressure to buy more than I need.
  10. Nutrition professional: That is the purpose: matching the plan to your circumstances and preferences.
A support service may not fit

A client checks a community food option before agreeing.

  1. Professional: Would you like information about the community pantry, including its actual opening hours?
  2. Client: Yes, but I need to know whether it is open outside my work shift.
  3. Professional: We should verify that rather than describe the service as accessible just because it is nearby.
  4. Client: I would also like to know what identification or eligibility information they request.
  5. Professional: I can check the published process without promising that every service uses the same rules.
  6. Client: If the hours do not fit, I would prefer another option instead of missing work.
  7. Professional: That is a practical constraint we should preserve when discussing possible support.
  8. Client: And I would like to choose whether to contact them myself or request an introduction.
  9. Professional: We can explain both routes where available and confirm your preference before sharing information.
  10. Client: Then getting information does not mean I have automatically enrolled or agreed to a referral.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Keep the rice; fix the logistics
  1. Read the corrected exchange and stress the constraints on space, equipment, and cost.
  2. Swap roles and repeat the two-choice comparison while preserving the client's preferred staple foods.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A lower unit price is not a lower bill

A small package costs $3; a larger package costs $8 and has a lower unit price. The client has $5 available for this purchase and cannot store the larger package safely.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "My available budget is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

D. five

The brief gives five dollars as the amount available for this purchase.

2. Professional: "The larger package costs ___ dollars upfront."

Answer and explanation

D. eight

Eight dollars exceeds the stated amount available at the checkout.

3. Client: "I also lack safe ___."

Answer and explanation

B. storage

The larger package cannot be stored safely in the supplied situation.

4. Professional: "The lower unit price does not settle ___."

Answer and explanation

C. feasibility

Cost at checkout and safe storage both affect whether the purchase is practical.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
