Lesson 03: Adapting plans to food access and culture
Original fictional case: Ana shares a kitchen and has one refrigerator shelf. She wants to keep rice and beans in her meals. Her food budget is limited, the nearest shop is a small market, and she has no freezer space. Nutrition professional Sam discusses two feasible shopping options with her. No clinical restriction or individualized nutrient target is supplied.
Language workshop: Keep the rice; fix the logistics
Communication goal: Negotiate feasible food options without dismissing cultural preferences or access constraints.
Language guidance: A cheaper large package is not necessarily the practical choice when storage is limited. Do not treat a preferred staple as the problem simply because it is unfamiliar to the professional.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which familiar foods would you like to keep? / What can you actually use in the shared kitchen? / Would there be space to store that after opening? / The unit price is lower, but the upfront cost is higher. / Which of these two options fits your week better? / A plan that needs a freezer would not fit your situation. / Would you like information about local food resources? / Let us check cost, storage, and preparation time together. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: food access: Practical ability to obtain suitable food. / food insecurity: Limited or uncertain access to adequate food. / food budget: Money available for food over a stated period. / staple food: Regularly eaten food central to a person's meals. / cultural preference: Food choice connected with personal or community traditions. / shared kitchen: Cooking space used by more than one household or person. / storage capacity: Space and conditions available for keeping food. / shelf-stable: Able to be stored unopened without refrigeration under stated conditions. / perishable: Likely to spoil unless stored and used appropriately. / batch cooking: Preparing several portions in one cooking session. / food waste: Food discarded rather than consumed. / unit price: Cost expressed for a standard quantity. / seasonal produce: Fruits or vegetables available in their relevant growing season. / substitution: Replacement chosen for a particular purpose or constraint. / preparation time: Time needed to make a food ready to eat. / equipment access: Availability of the tools needed for preparation. / community food resource: Local service supporting access to food. / acceptability: Whether an option fits the person's preferences and circumstances. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Discussing nutrient sources without a one-food solutionPlant-based does not describe the whole pattern+

Your goal

Explain nutrient-source terminology while distinguishing dietary preference from a verified deficiency.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Plant-based does not describe the whole pattern

Dev has recently started eating a fully plant-based diet and asks whether every plant-based drink has the same nutrients as dairy milk. Dietitian Noor compares two invented labels: Drink A lists added calcium and vitamin B12; Drink B lists neither. No blood results, symptoms, quantities consumed, or personal requirements are supplied. The conversation concerns sources and labels, not a diagnosis or supplement dose.

Dev: Client / Noor: Dietitian

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which product lists added calcium and vitamin B12?

Answer and explanation

D. Drink A

Only Drink A lists those added nutrients in the supplied labels.

2. What has not been supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. Individual nutrient requirements

The case contains label facts but no individualized requirement assessment.

3. What can the discussion establish from the labels alone?

Answer and explanation

A. The listed nutrient differences

The labels support a product comparison, not a clinical conclusion about Dev.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Fortified is a product description, not a promise that every nutritional need is met. Neither the dietary label nor the two drink labels establish a deficiency.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
plant-based pattern
Eating pattern centered on plant foods, with the exact exclusions needing clarification.clarify a plant-based pattern
vegan diet
Dietary pattern excluding animal-derived foods.assess a vegan diet
vegetarian diet
Pattern excluding meat, with other exclusions varying by type.clarify a vegetarian diet
macronutrient
Nutrient required in relatively large amounts, such as protein or carbohydrate.explain a macronutrient
micronutrient
Vitamin or mineral needed in relatively small amounts.identify a micronutrient
protein source
Food contributing protein to the diet.vary the protein sources
amino acid
Building block of proteins.explain an amino acid
legume
Plant food group including beans, peas, and lentils.include familiar legumes
fortification
Addition of nutrients to a food product.check for fortification
calcium
Mineral involved in bone structure and other body functions.identify a calcium source
vitamin B12
Vitamin needed for normal blood and nervous-system functions.identify a reliable vitamin B12 source
iron
Mineral involved in oxygen transport and other functions.discuss dietary iron sources
heme iron
Form of dietary iron found in animal tissues.distinguish heme iron
non-heme iron
Form of dietary iron found in plant foods and other sources.explain non-heme iron
bioavailability
Extent to which an ingested substance is available for use by the body.discuss nutrient bioavailability
nutrient adequacy
Whether intake meets relevant nutritional needs.assess nutrient adequacy
dietary variety
Range of foods included over time.explore dietary variety
enrichment
Addition of specified nutrients, often to restore amounts lost in processing.distinguish enrichment from a product claim
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Clarify actual foods, quantities, and any appropriate clinical information before interpreting adequacy. This exercise does not provide supplement recommendations or treatment instructions.

C Notice the language

A dietary label is not a complete assessment

Ask what a person actually eats. Vegan, vegetarian, and plant-based can describe different choices and do not establish adequacy or deficiency by themselves.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the pattern
What does plant-based mean in your meals?
Compare products
These drinks have different nutrient profiles despite similar packaging.
Explain fortification
This product has specified nutrients added to it.
Avoid a leap
That label difference does not diagnose a deficiency.
Ask for the actual item
Could we look at the brand and current label you use?
Broaden the account
Let us look at the whole pattern rather than one food.
Explain a source
A source contributes a nutrient; it does not automatically meet every need.
Preserve scope
Any individualized recommendation needs the appropriate assessment.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Contains versus meets needs
Contains identifies a nutrient in a product; meets needs requires a broader individualized assessment.
Source of
Source of identifies a contribution and should not be expanded into the only source of or a complete solution.
Not listed
Not listed describes the supplied label, not a laboratory test of the person's nutrient status.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Drink A lists added B12; Drink B does not

The supplied labels establish a difference between products, not Dev's nutritional status.

2. Which question clarifies the dietary label?

Answer and explanation

A. Which foods do you include and exclude?

Asking about actual foods clarifies what the person's dietary label means in practice.

3. What does bioavailability refer to?

Answer and explanation

C. Availability of an ingested substance for bodily use

Bioavailability concerns uptake and use, not shopping access or preference.

4. Which conclusion needs more information?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether the whole pattern meets Dev's needs

Assessing nutrient adequacy needs information beyond the two product descriptions supplied.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Plant-based does not describe the whole pattern

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dev: Client / Noor: Dietitian

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • vitamin B12
  • Bioavailability
  • legumes
  • dietary variety
  • vegan diet
  • plant-based pattern
  • protein source
  • Nutrient adequacy
  • micronutrient
  • fortification
  1. Dev
    I switched to plant-based meals, and I assumed these two drinks would be more or less interchangeable.
  2. Noor
    Before comparing them, could you explain your 1 and which foods you now include or leave out?
  3. Dev
    I no longer eat animal-derived foods. I use beans, lentils, grains, vegetables, and whichever drink is available.
  4. Noor
    That describes a 2. It tells me the exclusions, but not everything about quantities or nutrient sources.
  5. Dev
    I can see calcium on one label. Does that mean it occurs in all drinks made from plants?
  6. Noor
    No. This one lists 3, while the other label does not list those added nutrients.
  7. Dev
    So I should check the actual product, not treat the words plant-based as a complete nutrition statement.
  8. Noor
    Exactly. Drink A lists 4 and calcium; Drink B does not list either in the information we have.
  9. Dev
    Does choosing Drink B yesterday mean I have a deficiency, or is that jumping ahead of the evidence?
  10. Noor
    It is a jump. 5 needs an assessment of the broader pattern and any relevant clinical information.
  11. Dev
    People also tell me that only one particular food can supply enough protein. That sounds restrictive.
  12. Noor
    We can identify each 6 you actually eat before treating one food as the answer to every question.
  13. Dev
    Beans and lentils are already familiar to me. I would rather discuss those than an expensive specialty product.
  14. Noor
    Those are 7. Familiar foods are useful starting points when we discuss the variety of your meals.
  15. Dev
    I saw the word bioavailability in an article and thought it meant whether a shop had the product.
  16. Noor
    8 concerns how a substance becomes available for use by the body, not availability on a shelf.
  17. Dev
    Then two foods can contain a nutrient without every question about that nutrient being identical.
  18. Noor
    Right. We can explain that distinction without turning this label comparison into a claim about your 9 status or prescribing a supplement.
  19. Dev
    I will bring the labels I actually use and a record of my meals to the next appointment.
  20. Noor
    That will help us discuss 10 with real examples rather than assuming that every vegan diet looks the same.

Answers and explanations
  1. plant-based pattern Turn 2. A plant-based pattern can mean different food choices, so the professional asks which foods are actually included or excluded.
  2. vegan diet Turn 4. A vegan diet excludes animal-derived foods, but that label alone does not describe the person's complete nutrient intake.
  3. fortification Turn 6. Fortification is the addition of specified nutrients to a product and cannot be assumed for every product in the same category.
  4. vitamin B12 Turn 8. Vitamin B12 is one of the added nutrients explicitly listed for Drink A in this fictional comparison.
  5. Nutrient adequacy Turn 10. Nutrient adequacy concerns whether the overall intake meets relevant needs, which cannot be determined from one product choice alone.
  6. protein source Turn 12. A protein source contributes protein, but no single food must be assumed to describe the entire dietary pattern.
  7. legumes Turn 14. Legumes include beans and lentils, making the term directly relevant to the foods the client already eats.
  8. Bioavailability Turn 16. Bioavailability describes how an ingested substance becomes available for bodily use, not whether a retailer stocks a food.
  9. micronutrient Turn 18. A micronutrient is a vitamin or mineral needed in relatively small amounts; the conversation does not establish the client's status.
  10. dietary variety Turn 20. Dietary variety concerns the range of foods eaten over time and provides more context than a single dietary label.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dev: I switched to plant-based meals, and I assumed these two drinks would be more or less interchangeable.
  2. Noor: Before comparing them, could you explain your plant-based pattern and which foods you now include or leave out?
  3. Dev: I no longer eat animal-derived foods. I use beans, lentils, grains, vegetables, and whichever drink is available.
  4. Noor: That describes a vegan diet. It tells me the exclusions, but not everything about quantities or nutrient sources.
  5. Dev: I can see calcium on one label. Does that mean it occurs in all drinks made from plants?
  6. Noor: No. This one lists fortification, while the other label does not list those added nutrients.
  7. Dev: So I should check the actual product, not treat the words plant-based as a complete nutrition statement.
  8. Noor: Exactly. Drink A lists vitamin B12 and calcium; Drink B does not list either in the information we have.
  9. Dev: Does choosing Drink B yesterday mean I have a deficiency, or is that jumping ahead of the evidence?
  10. Noor: It is a jump. Nutrient adequacy needs an assessment of the broader pattern and any relevant clinical information.
  11. Dev: People also tell me that only one particular food can supply enough protein. That sounds restrictive.
  12. Noor: We can identify each protein source you actually eat before treating one food as the answer to every question.
  13. Dev: Beans and lentils are already familiar to me. I would rather discuss those than an expensive specialty product.
  14. Noor: Those are legumes. Familiar foods are useful starting points when we discuss the variety of your meals.
  15. Dev: I saw the word bioavailability in an article and thought it meant whether a shop had the product.
  16. Noor: Bioavailability concerns how a substance becomes available for use by the body, not availability on a shelf.
  17. Dev: Then two foods can contain a nutrient without every question about that nutrient being identical.
  18. Noor: Right. We can explain that distinction without turning this label comparison into a claim about your micronutrient status or prescribing a supplement.
  19. Dev: I will bring the labels I actually use and a record of my meals to the next appointment.
  20. Noor: That will help us discuss dietary variety with real examples rather than assuming that every vegan diet looks the same.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Plant-based means different exclusions

A professional clarifies a client's dietary description.

  1. Professional: When you say plant-based, which foods do you include and which do you leave out?
  2. Client: I mostly eat plants but still have eggs; I am not describing a fully vegan diet.
  3. Professional: That distinction matters. I should not replace your description with a stricter label.
  4. Client: I was concerned that the questionnaire only offered one box for several different patterns.
  5. Professional: We can record the actual foods rather than force an inaccurate label onto your routine.
  6. Client: Does the pattern's name tell you whether I get every nutrient I need?
  7. Professional: No. We need the broader intake and appropriate assessment, not the name alone.
  8. Client: I can bring examples of meals and the labels of products I use regularly.
  9. Professional: Those details will make the conversation more precise without assuming a deficiency.
  10. Client: Then I can explain my choices without defending a category I never actually selected.
Fortified does not mean identical

A client compares two drinks within the same product category.

  1. Client: Both cartons say oat drink, so I thought the nutrient profile would be identical.
  2. Dietitian: Products within that category can differ. Let us check the actual labels you brought.
  3. Client: This one lists added calcium; the other information does not list the same addition.
  4. Dietitian: That is a product difference we can describe without diagnosing anything about your intake.
  5. Client: Could I say that the first product automatically covers every nutritional need I have?
  6. Dietitian: No. A listed addition does not turn one product into a complete individualized diet.
  7. Client: I also need to say how much I actually drink rather than simply whether I buy it.
  8. Dietitian: Exactly. Product information and the amount consumed answer different parts of the assessment.
  9. Client: I will avoid describing all plant-based drinks as interchangeable in my summary.
  10. Dietitian: That keeps the wording tied to the products rather than an assumption about their category.
One nutrient does not define the whole meal

A client asks about protein in a familiar meal.

  1. Client: Someone dismissed my usual meal because it did not contain the protein product they sell.
  2. Dietitian: Let us identify the foods in your actual meal before accepting that conclusion.
  3. Client: It includes lentils, rice, vegetables, and ingredients I prepare in the usual family way.
  4. Dietitian: We can discuss those sources and quantities without assuming a branded product is necessary.
  5. Client: Would a protein discussion cover every question about the nutritional pattern at once?
  6. Dietitian: No. Protein is one part of the discussion; it does not describe every nutrient or need.
  7. Client: I would like an explanation that keeps my familiar foods in view.
  8. Dietitian: That is reasonable. A useful assessment starts with what you eat and what is feasible.
  9. Client: I can describe a few different days so the discussion is not based on one plate.
  10. Dietitian: That will help distinguish dietary variety from a single photographed or remembered meal.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Plant-based does not describe the whole pattern
  1. Read the exchange and distinguish contains, source, and meets needs.
  2. Swap roles and explain the difference between the two supplied labels without diagnosing a deficiency.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The brand changed

A client switches from a drink listing added B12 to a new brand whose label does not list B12. No symptoms or test results are supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "I changed the ___ I buy."

Answer and explanation

A. brand

The change concerns the actual product, not merely a different package size.

2. Dietitian: "We need to check its current ___."

Answer and explanation

C. label

The current label provides the product information needed for the comparison.

3. Client: "A nutrient difference does not establish a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. deficiency

No clinical assessment or test result establishes a deficiency in this case.

4. Dietitian: "We also need the wider dietary ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pattern

Overall intake provides context that cannot be supplied by one product label.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Professional audience: nutritionists, registered dietitians, nutrition educators, community nutrition staff
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. Lesson 04: Discussing nutrient sources without a one-food solution
Original fictional case: Dev has recently started eating a fully plant-based diet and asks whether every plant-based drink has the same nutrients as dairy milk. Dietitian Noor compares two invented labels: Drink A lists added calcium and vitamin B12; Drink B lists neither. No blood results, symptoms, quantities consumed, or personal requirements are supplied. The conversation concerns sources and labels, not a diagnosis or supplement dose.
Language workshop: Plant-based does not describe the whole pattern
Communication goal: Explain nutrient-source terminology while distinguishing dietary preference from a verified deficiency.
Language guidance: Fortified is a product description, not a promise that every nutritional need is met. Neither the dietary label nor the two drink labels establish a deficiency.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What does plant-based mean in your meals? / These drinks have different nutrient profiles despite similar packaging. / This product has specified nutrients added to it. / That label difference does not diagnose a deficiency. / Could we look at the brand and current label you use? / Let us look at the whole pattern rather than one food. / A source contributes a nutrient; it does not automatically meet every need. / Any individualized recommendation needs the appropriate assessment. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: plant-based pattern: Eating pattern centered on plant foods, with the exact exclusions needing clarification. / vegan diet: Dietary pattern excluding animal-derived foods. / vegetarian diet: Pattern excluding meat, with other exclusions varying by type. / macronutrient: Nutrient required in relatively large amounts, such as protein or carbohydrate. / micronutrient: Vitamin or mineral needed in relatively small amounts. / protein source: Food contributing protein to the diet. / amino acid: Building block of proteins. / legume: Plant food group including beans, peas, and lentils. / fortification: Addition of nutrients to a food product. / calcium: Mineral involved in bone structure and other body functions. / vitamin B12: Vitamin needed for normal blood and nervous-system functions. / iron: Mineral involved in oxygen transport and other functions. / heme iron: Form of dietary iron found in animal tissues. / non-heme iron: Form of dietary iron found in plant foods and other sources. / bioavailability: Extent to which an ingested substance is available for use by the body. / nutrient adequacy: Whether intake meets relevant nutritional needs. / dietary variety: Range of foods included over time. / enrichment: Addition of specified nutrients, often to restore amounts lost in processing. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Clarifying a clinical referral and assessmentA flagged result is not a complete explanation+

Your goal

Distinguish a referral question, a reported laboratory flag, and the conclusions an assessment can support.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A flagged result is not a complete explanation

Dr Chen refers Morgan to registered dietitian Ellis for a nutrition assessment after an electronic result is flagged outside its laboratory reference range. The actual test, value, diagnosis, and treatment plan are not supplied. Morgan asks whether the flag proves poor eating caused the result. Ellis requests the authorized referral information and explains the nutrition assessment while leaving medical interpretation to the appropriate clinician.

Morgan: Client / Ellis: Registered dietitian

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the supplied result establish?

Answer and explanation

D. A laboratory flag was reported

Only a reported flag is supplied; the actual test and interpretation are absent.

2. Who requested the nutrition assessment?

Answer and explanation

B. Dr Chen

Dr Chen is explicitly named as the referring clinician in the case.

3. Which question requires appropriate clinical interpretation?

Answer and explanation

D. What the flagged test means medically

Interpreting an unspecified test medically requires the actual result and qualified clinical review.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A laboratory flag does not establish a dietary cause. Nutrition and medical diagnoses are not interchangeable labels; actual assessment and professional authority matter.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
referral question
Specific issue the referring professional asks another service to address.clarify the referral question
nutrition assessment
Systematic collection and interpretation of nutrition-related information.complete a nutrition assessment
medical nutrition therapy
Clinical nutrition services for a condition within an appropriately qualified professional's scope.explain medical nutrition therapy
laboratory reference range
Comparison interval supplied by a laboratory for a test.check the laboratory reference range
abnormal flag
Marker that a reported result falls outside a stated reference or alert criterion.clarify an abnormal flag
biochemical data
Laboratory information considered in an assessment.review biochemical data
anthropometric data
Body measurements considered with their context and limitations.interpret anthropometric data
clinical interpretation
Qualified explanation of findings in the person's clinical context.request clinical interpretation
nutrition diagnosis
Nutrition-related problem identified through an appropriate assessment framework.distinguish a nutrition diagnosis
medical diagnosis
Identification of a disease or clinical condition by an authorized professional.distinguish a medical diagnosis
etiology
Cause or contributing origin of a problem, when established.avoid assuming etiology
signs and symptoms
Observed findings and reported experiences relevant to assessment.distinguish signs and symptoms
care coordination
Organization of information and responsibilities across services.support care coordination
informed permission
Agreement after the relevant purpose and process have been explained.seek informed permission
record access
Authorized ability to view relevant information.confirm record access
assessment finding
Result established through the actual assessment process.document an assessment finding
clinical context
Relevant health circumstances needed to interpret information.preserve clinical context
follow-up question
Unresolved issue requiring a later response or review.assign a follow-up question
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

Do not insert a plausible test, value, or disease to make the case sound complete. The missing information is precisely what the referral discussion must identify.

C Notice the language

A referral is a question to clarify

Do not present the client as a problem to fix. Explain why information is being collected and which professional answers each question.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the purpose
What question did the referring clinician ask us to address?
Avoid blame
The flag does not show that you caused the result through food choices.
Identify missing data
I do not yet have the actual result and its clinical context.
Explain assessment
We will review relevant intake, history, and available information.
Separate roles
Your clinician needs to explain the medical interpretation.
Seek permission
May I request the relevant information through the authorized route?
Limit conclusions
That conclusion would go beyond the information we have.
Assign follow-up
Let us confirm who will answer the outstanding test question.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Because versus after
After states sequence; because asserts a causal connection. A referral after a result does not establish its cause.
Flagged versus diagnosed
Flagged describes a laboratory marker; diagnosed describes a clinical conclusion requiring appropriate evidence.
Nutrition versus medical
The two modifiers identify different professional meanings and must not be silently dropped.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence avoids an unsupported cause?

Answer and explanation

A. You were referred after a flagged result

After preserves the sequence without asserting an unestablished dietary cause.

2. What should Ellis request?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual referral question and authorized relevant information

The missing referral detail and actual record are needed before interpreting the assessment task.

3. Which distinction should remain explicit?

Answer and explanation

A. Nutrition diagnosis and medical diagnosis

Those diagnostic terms concern different professional meanings and cannot be used interchangeably.

4. Which reply best addresses Morgan's concern?

Answer and explanation

C. The flag alone does not tell us that food caused it

The reply rejects unsupported blame without denying that nutrition may be relevant after assessment.

E Conversations

A flagged result is not a complete explanation

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Client / Ellis: Registered dietitian

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • assessment finding
  • Etiology
  • care coordination
  • nutrition assessment
  • record access
  • referral question
  • clinical interpretation
  • laboratory reference range
  • abnormal flag
  • nutrition diagnosis
  1. Morgan
    My result was flagged, and the referral came straight afterward. Does that mean I caused it by eating badly?
  2. Ellis
    An 1 does not tell us that. I also do not yet have the actual test or its context.
  3. Morgan
    I only saw a colored mark in the portal. Nobody has explained what the test was measuring.
  4. Ellis
    We need the 2 from the appropriate clinician, rather than guessing from the color of a portal message.
  5. Morgan
    Then what would happen in this appointment, and how is it different from repeating that medical explanation?
  6. Ellis
    A 3 looks at relevant intake, history, and available information within my professional role.
  7. Morgan
    Should I bring every document I have, or is there a particular question you need answered?
  8. Ellis
    First I will clarify the 4 so we know what Dr Chen is asking this service to address.
  9. Morgan
    The portal mentions a reference range. I thought anything outside it automatically named a disease.
  10. Ellis
    A 5 is a comparison interval, not a complete diagnosis. The actual result needs proper clinical context.
  11. Morgan
    Could we request the information instead of my trying to remember a number I did not understand?
  12. Ellis
    Yes, through authorized 6 and the appropriate permission process. We should not ask you to invent a missing value.
  13. Morgan
    I have also seen nutrition diagnosis in a form. Is that simply another name for a medical diagnosis?
  14. Ellis
    No. A 7 has a specific nutrition-assessment meaning; the two terms are not interchangeable.
  15. Morgan
    So we should not label the cause before either professional has reviewed the information needed for their role.
  16. Ellis
    Correct. 8 should not be assumed simply because the nutrition appointment followed the flagged result.
  17. Morgan
    I would like one clear route for the unanswered medical question, rather than being passed between services.
  18. Ellis
    That is part of 9. We can confirm which clinician will explain the test and how the response reaches you.
  19. Morgan
    Then today we can clarify the assessment without describing a disease or diet that has not been established.
  20. Ellis
    Exactly. We will document each actual 10 and keep the outstanding questions visible until the right professional answers them.

Answers and explanations
  1. abnormal flag Turn 2. An abnormal flag marks a result against a laboratory criterion but does not establish a dietary cause or diagnosis by itself.
  2. clinical interpretation Turn 4. Clinical interpretation explains the actual finding in the person's health context and requires appropriate professional review.
  3. nutrition assessment Turn 6. A nutrition assessment collects and interprets relevant nutrition information rather than replacing medical interpretation of an unspecified test.
  4. referral question Turn 8. The referral question states what the referring professional wants the receiving service to assess or address.
  5. laboratory reference range Turn 10. A laboratory reference range is a comparison interval and does not independently supply a complete clinical diagnosis.
  6. record access Turn 12. Record access must be authorized and directed toward the relevant information rather than unrestricted sharing of personal records.
  7. nutrition diagnosis Turn 14. A nutrition diagnosis identifies a nutrition-related problem through an appropriate assessment framework and is not interchangeable with a medical diagnosis.
  8. Etiology Turn 16. Etiology concerns the cause or origin of a problem, which has not been established by the referral sequence in this case.
  9. care coordination Turn 18. Care coordination organizes responsibilities across services so an unanswered clinical question has an appropriate owner and response route.
  10. assessment finding Turn 20. An assessment finding must come from the assessment performed, not from invented details added to fill gaps in the referral.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: My result was flagged, and the referral came straight afterward. Does that mean I caused it by eating badly?
  2. Ellis: An abnormal flag does not tell us that. I also do not yet have the actual test or its context.
  3. Morgan: I only saw a colored mark in the portal. Nobody has explained what the test was measuring.
  4. Ellis: We need the clinical interpretation from the appropriate clinician, rather than guessing from the color of a portal message.
  5. Morgan: Then what would happen in this appointment, and how is it different from repeating that medical explanation?
  6. Ellis: A nutrition assessment looks at relevant intake, history, and available information within my professional role.
  7. Morgan: Should I bring every document I have, or is there a particular question you need answered?
  8. Ellis: First I will clarify the referral question so we know what Dr Chen is asking this service to address.
  9. Morgan: The portal mentions a reference range. I thought anything outside it automatically named a disease.
  10. Ellis: A laboratory reference range is a comparison interval, not a complete diagnosis. The actual result needs proper clinical context.
  11. Morgan: Could we request the information instead of my trying to remember a number I did not understand?
  12. Ellis: Yes, through authorized record access and the appropriate permission process. We should not ask you to invent a missing value.
  13. Morgan: I have also seen nutrition diagnosis in a form. Is that simply another name for a medical diagnosis?
  14. Ellis: No. A nutrition diagnosis has a specific nutrition-assessment meaning; the two terms are not interchangeable.
  15. Morgan: So we should not label the cause before either professional has reviewed the information needed for their role.
  16. Ellis: Correct. Etiology should not be assumed simply because the nutrition appointment followed the flagged result.
  17. Morgan: I would like one clear route for the unanswered medical question, rather than being passed between services.
  18. Ellis: That is part of care coordination. We can confirm which clinician will explain the test and how the response reaches you.
  19. Morgan: Then today we can clarify the assessment without describing a disease or diet that has not been established.
  20. Ellis: Exactly. We will document each actual assessment finding and keep the outstanding questions visible until the right professional answers them.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The referral is for assessment, not a settled plan

A dietitian checks a referrer's request.

  1. Dietitian: Your referral asks for a nutrition assessment, but the attached message sounds like treatment is already agreed.
  2. Referrer: The assessment is requested; no individualized nutrition plan has been finalized through this referral.
  3. Dietitian: I will make that distinction explicit so the client does not hear a proposal as a completed decision.
  4. Referrer: Please also let us know what information is missing for your actual assessment role.
  5. Dietitian: I need the relevant referral question and authorized clinical information, not a guessed diagnosis.
  6. Referrer: We will provide those through the approved route and identify the clinician for medical questions.
  7. Dietitian: That will avoid asking the client to resolve contradictions between incomplete documents.
  8. Referrer: Agreed. The receiving service should not have to infer the purpose from a broad heading alone.
  9. Dietitian: I will acknowledge receipt separately from confirming that the assessment has been completed.
  10. Referrer: That gives both teams a clear account of what is requested and what remains pending.
A body measurement needs context

A client asks whether one number settles the assessment.

  1. Client: I saw a single body measurement on the form and thought it would determine the whole appointment.
  2. Dietitian: One measurement does not replace the wider assessment or the context needed to interpret it.
  3. Client: I would like to understand why it is requested before deciding what information to provide.
  4. Dietitian: We should explain the purpose, relevant limitations, and actual consent process clearly.
  5. Client: Could a copied number be inaccurate if the method or date is not clear?
  6. Dietitian: Yes. The source, timing, and method matter when interpreting recorded information.
  7. Client: I do not want an old estimate to be presented as a current measurement.
  8. Dietitian: Then we should identify it accurately and avoid increasing its certainty in the record.
  9. Client: The discussion should still include my routine and the practical questions I came with.
  10. Dietitian: Exactly. A number is part of information gathering, not a substitute for listening.
After the result is not because of the diet

A client checks a causal misunderstanding.

  1. Client: The appointment came after the flagged test, so I assumed my meals had caused the result.
  2. Dietitian: The timing of the referral does not establish that cause by itself.
  3. Client: Then saying after the test is different from saying because of my eating pattern.
  4. Dietitian: Yes. Because makes a causal claim that would need appropriate evidence and interpretation.
  5. Client: I only have a portal notification, not an explanation of the actual test.
  6. Dietitian: The relevant clinician should explain the result in context rather than leave you to guess.
  7. Client: Can we record that question so it does not disappear between the two services?
  8. Dietitian: We can identify the responsible contact and the route for the missing explanation.
  9. Client: Meanwhile, the nutrition discussion can use the facts available without inventing a disease.
  10. Dietitian: Correct. The assessment should not become more certain than the information supports.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A flagged result is not a complete explanation
  1. Read the corrected dialogue and emphasize after rather than because when describing the referral.
  2. Swap roles and distinguish the assessment purpose from the unresolved medical interpretation.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A referral lacks its question

A referral says nutrition review but provides no question or actual test results. The receiving service requests clarification from the referrer through the authorized channel.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Receiver: "The referral question is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. missing

The words nutrition review do not specify the question to be addressed.

2. Referrer: "I will provide the relevant test ___."

Answer and explanation

C. information

The actual relevant information is needed rather than a guessed result.

3. Receiver: "We will use the authorized ___."

Answer and explanation

C. channel

The scenario explicitly requires an authorized route for sharing relevant information.

4. Referrer: "The referral alone is not a confirmed ___."

Answer and explanation

A. diagnosis

The incomplete referral does not independently establish any named clinical condition.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. REFERENCE
Course: Nutrition and Dietetics English
Professional audience: nutritionists, registered dietitians, nutrition educators, community nutrition staff
Scope: All people, organizations, cases, figures, and documents in the exercises are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, a diet prescription, or clinical certification. Titles, credentials, and permission to provide medical nutrition therapy differ by jurisdiction. Use qualified clinical review for disease treatment, suspected deficiency, allergies, eating disorders, supplement interactions, and urgent symptoms. Do not calculate a dose or infer a diagnosis from these exercises. Protect client information and follow actual local procedures.
Lesson 05: Clarifying a clinical referral and assessment
Original fictional case: Dr Chen refers Morgan to registered dietitian Ellis for a nutrition assessment after an electronic result is flagged outside its laboratory reference range. The actual test, value, diagnosis, and treatment plan are not supplied. Morgan asks whether the flag proves poor eating caused the result. Ellis requests the authorized referral information and explains the nutrition assessment while leaving medical interpretation to the appropriate clinician.
Language workshop: A flagged result is not a complete explanation
Communication goal: Distinguish a referral question, a reported laboratory flag, and the conclusions an assessment can support.
Language guidance: A laboratory flag does not establish a dietary cause. Nutrition and medical diagnoses are not interchangeable labels; actual assessment and professional authority matter.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What question did the referring clinician ask us to address? / The flag does not show that you caused the result through food choices. / I do not yet have the actual result and its clinical context. / We will review relevant intake, history, and available information. / Your clinician needs to explain the medical interpretation. / May I request the relevant information through the authorized route? / That conclusion would go beyond the information we have. / Let us confirm who will answer the outstanding test question. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: referral question: Specific issue the referring professional asks another service to address. / nutrition assessment: Systematic collection and interpretation of nutrition-related information. / medical nutrition therapy: Clinical nutrition services for a condition within an appropriately qualified professional's scope. / laboratory reference range: Comparison interval supplied by a laboratory for a test. / abnormal flag: Marker that a reported result falls outside a stated reference or alert criterion. / biochemical data: Laboratory information considered in an assessment. / anthropometric data: Body measurements considered with their context and limitations. / clinical interpretation: Qualified explanation of findings in the person's clinical context. / nutrition diagnosis: Nutrition-related problem identified through an appropriate assessment framework. / medical diagnosis: Identification of a disease or clinical condition by an authorized professional. / etiology: Cause or contributing origin of a problem, when established. / signs and symptoms: Observed findings and reported experiences relevant to assessment. / care coordination: Organization of information and responsibilities across services. / informed permission: Agreement after the relevant purpose and process have been explained. / record access: Authorized ability to view relevant information. / assessment finding: Result established through the actual assessment process. / clinical context: Relevant health circumstances needed to interpret information. / follow-up question: Unresolved issue requiring a later response or review. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue and emphasize after rather than because when describing the referral. Swap roles and distinguish the assessment purpose from the unresolved medical interpretation. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Lesson 05: Clarifying a clinical referral and assessment
Original fictional case: Dr Chen refers Morgan to registered dietitian Ellis for a nutrition assessment after an electronic result is flagged outside its laboratory reference range. The actual test, value, diagnosis, and treatment plan are not supplied. Morgan asks whether the flag proves poor eating caused the result. Ellis requests the authorized referral information and explains the nutrition assessment while leaving medical interpretation to the appropriate clinician.
Language workshop: A flagged result is not a complete explanation
Communication goal: Distinguish a referral question, a reported laboratory flag, and the conclusions an assessment can support.
Language guidance: A laboratory flag does not establish a dietary cause. Nutrition and medical diagnoses are not interchangeable labels; actual assessment and professional authority matter.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What question did the referring clinician ask us to address? / The flag does not show that you caused the result through food choices. / I do not yet have the actual result and its clinical context. / We will review relevant intake, history, and available information. / Your clinician needs to explain the medical interpretation. / May I request the relevant information through the authorized route? / That conclusion would go beyond the information we have. / Let us confirm who will answer the outstanding test question. / Are you asking about the finding or the next step? / Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail. / That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed. / That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome. / That decision sits outside my authority. / Who is responsible for that specific next step? / Let me restate the distinction more clearly. / We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
Vocabulary: referral question: Specific issue the referring professional asks another service to address. / nutrition assessment: Systematic collection and interpretation of nutrition-related information. / medical nutrition therapy: Clinical nutrition services for a condition within an appropriately qualified professional's scope. / laboratory reference range: Comparison interval supplied by a laboratory for a test. / abnormal flag: Marker that a reported result falls outside a stated reference or alert criterion. / biochemical data: Laboratory information considered in an assessment. / anthropometric data: Body measurements considered with their context and limitations. / clinical interpretation: Qualified explanation of findings in the person's clinical context. / nutrition diagnosis: Nutrition-related problem identified through an appropriate assessment framework. / medical diagnosis: Identification of a disease or clinical condition by an authorized professional. / etiology: Cause or contributing origin of a problem, when established. / signs and symptoms: Observed findings and reported experiences relevant to assessment. / care coordination: Organization of information and responsibilities across services. / informed permission: Agreement after the relevant purpose and process have been explained. / record access: Authorized ability to view relevant information. / assessment finding: Result established through the actual assessment process. / clinical context: Relevant health circumstances needed to interpret information. / follow-up question: Unresolved issue requiring a later response or review. / clarification request: Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction. / source attribution: Identification of where information came from. / working assumption: Provisional premise used while information is incomplete. / scope boundary: Limit of the work or authority being discussed. / action owner: Named person responsible for a specified next step. / check-back: Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue and emphasize after rather than because when describing the referral. Swap roles and distinguish the assessment purpose from the unresolved medical interpretation. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied choices. Check the explanations, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Morgan: Client / Ellis: Registered dietitian
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A referral says nutrition review but provides no question or actual test results. The receiving service requests clarification from the referrer through the authorized channel.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: I would like one clear route for the unanswered medical question, rather than being passed between services.
Ellis: That is part of care coordination. We can confirm which clinician will explain the test and how the response reaches you.
Morgan: Then today we can clarify the assessment without describing a disease or diet that has not been established.
Ellis: Exactly. We will document each actual assessment finding and keep the outstanding questions visible until the right professional answers them.
END REFERENCE
06Handling supplement claims and interaction questionsNatural is not an interaction check+

Your goal

Explain evidence limits and route product-specific safety questions without endorsing an advertising claim.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Natural is not an interaction check

Client Alex brings an online advertisement for a supplement blend described as natural and clinically tested. The advertisement does not identify the tested formulation, study population, or outcomes. Alex also takes a prescription medicine, but no medicine name or supplement dose is supplied. Nutrition professional Quinn asks for the actual product information and arranges appropriate pharmacist or prescriber review; no stop, start, or dose instruction is given.

Alex: Client / Quinn: Nutrition professional

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is missing from the advertisement?

Answer and explanation

C. Details of the tested formulation and outcomes

The brief states that essential details behind the testing claim are absent.

2. Why is an interaction review needed?

Answer and explanation

D. Alex also takes a prescription medicine

Product and medicine details need qualified review; the advertising language cannot establish compatibility.

3. Which instruction is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A stop or start direction

No treatment or supplement change is authorized by the facts of this exercise.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Clinically tested does not state what was tested or whether a meaningful benefit was demonstrated. Natural does not establish safety, and a quality-testing mark does not prove clinical effectiveness.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
dietary supplement
Product intended to supplement the diet under the relevant regulatory definition.discuss a dietary supplement
Supplement Facts panel
US label panel listing specified supplement ingredients and amounts.inspect the Supplement Facts panel
active ingredient
Ingredient intended to produce a relevant effect.identify an active ingredient
proprietary blend
Mixture marketed under a product-specific blend designation.clarify a proprietary blend
formulation
Specific composition and form of a product.compare the tested formulation
health claim
Statement connecting a product or substance with a health-related outcome under applicable rules.examine a health claim
structure/function claim
Claim about supporting a body structure or function under applicable labeling rules.distinguish a structure/function claim
evidence quality
Strength and reliability of the information supporting a conclusion.assess evidence quality
study population
People included in a research study.identify the study population
outcome measure
Defined result used to evaluate a study or intervention.clarify the outcome measure
randomized trial
Study assigning participants to groups through a random process.describe a randomized trial
systematic review
Structured synthesis of relevant research using explicit methods.consult a systematic review
interaction
Effect of one substance or treatment on another's action or safety.check for an interaction
adverse effect
Unwanted harmful effect associated with a product or intervention.report an adverse effect
contraindication
Circumstance making an intervention inappropriate under the relevant clinical assessment.verify a contraindication
product variability
Differences in composition or characteristics between products or batches.acknowledge product variability
third-party testing
Testing conducted by an organization separate from the manufacturer.interpret third-party testing
pharmacist review
Assessment by a pharmacist of relevant medicine or product questions.request pharmacist review
clarification request
Question that resolves an unclear meaning or instruction.make a targeted clarification request
source attribution
Identification of where information came from.preserve source attribution
working assumption
Provisional premise used while information is incomplete.challenge a working assumption
scope boundary
Limit of the work or authority being discussed.state the scope boundary
action owner
Named person responsible for a specified next step.confirm the action owner
check-back
Restatement used to verify that a message was understood.complete a check-back

The actual product, medicine, and clinical circumstances are needed for interaction review. Do not use the exercise to start, stop, or change a supplement or prescription.

C Notice the language

Respect the question without endorsing the claim

A person may have invested money or hope in a product. Acknowledge the question, then examine what the claim actually establishes.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge interest
I can see why that claim caught your attention.
Ask for specifics
Which formulation was tested, and in whom?
Separate evidence
Being studied is not the same as showing a useful benefit.
Clarify safety
Natural does not tell us whether an interaction is possible.
Check the actual product
Could we review the full label rather than the advertisement alone?
Explain certification limits
A quality check does not establish effectiveness for your situation.
Route the question
The pharmacist or prescriber needs the exact product and medicine details.
Avoid an instruction
I am not giving a start, stop, or dose direction here.
Clarify the question
Are you asking about the finding or the next step?
Invite correction
Please correct me if I have misunderstood that detail.
Attribute information
That is what the current record says; it has not been independently confirmed.
Separate possibility
That is a possibility, not an agreed outcome.
Check scope
That decision sits outside my authority.
Pin down responsibility
Who is responsible for that specific next step?
Repair misunderstanding
Let me restate the distinction more clearly.
Close an exchange
We agree on the action, the owner, and the point for review.
6 language and precision notes
Tested versus effective
Tested says an investigation occurred; effective requires evidence of the relevant benefit in the relevant context.
May interact
May flags a possibility for review without claiming that an interaction has already been demonstrated in this client.
This formulation
This formulation narrows the statement to the actual product composition, not every item with a similar marketing name.
Reported versus verified
Reported attributes information to a source; verified indicates it has been checked. Do not use the words interchangeably.
Could versus will
Could introduces a possibility. Will commits to an action or predicts an outcome; check whether that certainty is justified.
By versus at
By gives a latest time; at identifies a scheduled moment. State the time zone when people work across locations.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question best evaluates clinically tested?

Answer and explanation

C. What formulation, population, and outcome were studied?

The tested product, participants, and outcomes are needed to understand what the claim supports.

2. What does natural establish here?

Answer and explanation

D. An advertising description, not verified compatibility

The word natural does not supply the missing safety or interaction assessment.

3. Which distinction is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Quality testing and clinical effectiveness answer different questions

Product-quality checks do not themselves establish clinical benefit or individual compatibility.

4. Which next step stays within the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Obtain product details for appropriate pharmacist or prescriber review

The scenario calls for qualified review using actual details, not a treatment instruction.
